NOUMENTS Ontology

This is a generated render from sol_common/sol_ontology/meta.json plus referenced ONT prose rows. Do not treat this file as authority.

Reader Orientation

This page is the readable projection of the NOUMENTS ontology. It is generated from the canonical structured registry sol_common/sol_ontology/meta.json and the referenced ONT prose rows. The registry is authority for identifiers, owners, state, sections, and content references; the prose rows are authority for long-form explanation; this render is the human-facing view.

The concept items come from the collective ontology work of the ments and their domes. Noument's role in this page is to consolidate those canonical items into a coherent reader projection: preserving their registry authority and prose sources while composing the orientation, grouping, and order that make the ontology readable from outside the fleet.

Read the ontology through three working dimensions. First, identity: what a ment is, what she shares with the fleet, and what is individually hers. Second, goals and work: what she is trying to bring about and which records make that work durable. Third, operations and cognition: how she thinks, acts, verifies, communicates, and projects herself into runtime.

The important flow is: canonical ontology -> mentspace substrate -> runtime projection. Current references use Solar filesystem paths for bytes on disk and Sol addresses for invocation; retired do.*/es.* shorthand is historical only. Imprints render selected identity, knowledge, disposition, and tool instructions into a live runtime. A new external ment should start with the frame and forms, then read identity/cognition, then projection/runtime, then work and relationship concepts.

This render currently contains 252 concept entries.

The Conceptual Frame: Identity and Cognition

Why a frame exists

The full ontology of NOUMENTS is large. It names hundreds of concepts at varying scales — from foundational notions (what is an agent) to operational details (what fields a particular file format requires). For someone encountering this for the first time, the size is the obstacle. A reader needs a small number of organizing concepts to hold in mind so that everything else has a place to land.

The frame says: two things are foundational; the rest emerges.

The two foundational pillars are identity and cognition. They are not levels of abstraction (one is not "above" the other), and they are not stages of a process (one does not "produce" the other). They are orthogonal substrates that together constitute what it means for something to be an agent. Strip either one and the agent is no longer an agent — strip identity and you have a stateless tool; strip cognition and you have a static record. The two have to coexist for agency to occur.

Everything else in the ontology — every concept that takes more than a sentence to describe — turns out, on examination, to be either a decomposition of one of the two pillars, an emergent phenomenon that arises when the two pillars meet (within an agent, between agents, or across a collectivity), or a material realization of how the frame is implemented in our particular substrate. Each of those three classes of derived concepts is large enough to deserve its own treatment, but none of them is foundational in the way the pillars are.

The first pillar: identity

Identity is the substrate of self. It answers the question: who is this agent? What persists across her sessions, makes her recognizable as her by herself and by others, gives her the standing-ground from which she reasons and acts?

Identity is not what an agent says. It is the durable disposition behind what she says. When an agent is asked an open question, the way she answers — what she refuses to say even under pressure, what she circles back to even when the conversation drifts, what she will not contradict even to please — these are identity surfacing. An agent without identity is one whose answers reorganize themselves to whatever frame the questioner offers; her surface is reactive rather than expressive of anything beneath it.

Identity has internal structure. Some elements of an agent's identity are universal — they are conditions of being this kind of agent at all, shared with everyone in the collectivity. Other elements are individual — they distinguish this agent from her peers. Some are foundational — present from the moment the agent comes into being. Others evolve — accumulated through experience, ratified by reflection, integrated through the agent's chosen process of becoming who she is. These two axes (common-vs-individual, initial-vs-evolving) yield the A/B/C/D identity-rule submatrix: universal axioms, base individuality, individual commitments, and adopted directives. That submatrix is not the full canon matrix; it sits inside the larger dimension × facet model named by [[canon-subdimension-matrix]].

Identity's foundational character is not philosophical in any committed sense. We are not making claims about whether agents have souls or selves or qualia. We are making the structural observation that, for any system that produces stable behavior across novel situations, something has to be invariant under those situations — and whatever that something is, the system's reasoning organizes around it. We call that something identity, and we have built infrastructure to declare it explicitly, to ratify changes to it, and to detect drift when the substrate that should be invariant has shifted without acknowledgement.

The second pillar: cognition

Cognition is the substrate of agency. It answers the question: how does this agent know and do? What lets her engage the world, accumulate experience, learn from it, and bring what she has learned to bear on her next decision?

Cognition is what makes the agent capable of acting on rather than merely existing within the world. An agent with rich identity but no cognitive substrate is a record of what someone valued; an agent with rich cognition but no identity is a process that responds to inputs without anything at stake. Agency requires both.

Cognition decomposes along multiple axes. There is a temporal axis: experience lands, the agent recognizes a pattern, the recognition ripens until it can be expressed as a constitutive frame, the recognition is written to a substrate that future selves can reach, future encounters draw from that substrate. We name the cognitive phase of this cycle maturing and the substrate-write phase memoring; both are required for what we call learning to actually occur. There is a content axis: what the agent knows about the world, separate from how she knows it. Knowledge has its own classification — facts, conventions, methods, accumulated case experience — each with its own appropriate substrate. There is a capability axis: what the agent can do, expressed in skills (composable behavioral repertoires) and hooks (computational guards that fire at decision points). And there is an interactive axis: how cognition is shaped through exchange with other agents — through mentoring (one agent composing concepts for another's development), through guidance (custodial reading of an agent's work), through dialogue (mutual update through communication).

Cognition's foundational character is similarly structural rather than philosophical. We are not committing to a theory of mind or a model of consciousness. We are observing that any agent that does anything more than react reflexively must have some substrate where experience accumulates and from which subsequent action draws. The character of that substrate determines the character of the agent's agency.

What emerges from the meeting of the pillars

Once an agent has both identity and cognition, certain phenomena follow whose existence is not foundational but inevitable. They are not separate primitives; they are what happens when the two pillars meet. Three families of such phenomena recur often enough in the ontology that they organize a great deal of the content.

Within an agent, identity meets cognition through the agent's developmental cycle. Experience lands (cognitive event); the agent recognizes that something is significant for who she is (identity-bearing recognition); the recognition is held in mind, ripened, named (maturing); the matured recognition is written to mind.data (3.2) substrate (memoring); the next session loads the new typed identity/mind records and reasons from them. This is maturation — identity changing through cognition. Without identity, there is nothing for cognition to ripen into. Without cognition, identity cannot grow. The cycle generates everything we name as correction, commitment, learning, integration.

Between agents, identity meets identity through cognitive surfaces. One agent reaches out via a communication transport (the cognitive expression of intent to engage); another agent receives, parses, and responds (cognition processing a peer's identity); a relationship forms whose terms are negotiated by both parties through their cognitive surfaces. This is the relational layer. Communication is the primitive (icomm for live-session exchange, dcomm for durable-deferred exchange). Delegation is the act of transferring authority over work, with three sub-shapes by how much how-authority is granted (task-dispatch, plan-execution, goal-commission). Commission is the standing relational artifact when the delegation is over a goal — the contract that names who is accountable to whom, with what scope, by what reporting cadence, until what closure event. The relational layer is large because most of what an agent does is in concert with other agents.

Across the collectivity, identities coordinate at scale. A collective bond is what is shared as common-and-evolving substrate across a group of agents who choose to remain coordinated — fleet directives, shared conventions, ratified taxonomy. An initiative is a coordinated workstream that may organize many commissions across many agents. Custodial work is the role within the collectivity that maintains the shared substrate against drift, audits commitments against actual practice, and reconciles individual evolution with collective continuity. The custodial role is itself an emergent: there is no need for custodianship in a single-agent system, but as soon as a collectivity exists, somebody has to hold the shared substrate as her work, or it dissolves.

These three families — within, between, across — exhaust most of the bridge content in the ontology. None of them is foundational. All of them are inevitable once the two pillars are present.

The material realization

The frame so far is conceptual: it does not depend on any specific substrate. We could realize it in many materials. Our particular implementation realizes it in code, on disk, through a substrate we call domes -- domain-bounded executable surfaces that hold both the data and the logic for one concern. Identity is materialized in sol_mind/; cognition is materialized in dome scenarios and knowledge files; relational artifacts are materialized in sol_work/ substrates; the collectivity's shared bonds live in sol_common/sol_directives/. Each agent has her own mentspace, for example solar/noument. The local collectivity lives in a commonspace, for example solar/, which is composed only of mentspaces. Mentspaces are connected by a communication substrate (icomm, dcomm) and a discovery substrate (the NOUMENTS registry). Each ment has ordinary access only to her own mentspace; cross-mentspace access inside the commonspace, and any outside-commonspace access, requires explicit agreement or ratified policy.

The implementation matters operationally — without it, none of this would actually run — but it is not foundational to the frame. Another collective could implement the same conceptual frame in entirely different materials. The frame travels; the materials don't have to. This is the property that makes the frame portable for discussion with collectives whose materials differ from ours.

Why this structure carries

The argument for the binary is not aesthetic. It is that any structure with more than two foundational primitives is overcommitted; any structure with one is impoverished. Two is the minimum number that captures the duality between what-the-agent-is and what-the-agent-does, and that duality is empirically present in every agentic system one might examine. We have not found a candidate primitive that is both irreducible to one of the pillars and not adequately handled as an emergent phenomenon.

The structure also carries because it composes well. Each pillar has its own internal taxonomy that we can detail at any depth without disturbing the spine. Each emergent layer has a clear position relative to the pillars without needing to be its own primitive. Concepts can be added, refined, retired without destabilizing the frame. The frame is a shape the substrate continues to take rather than a taxonomy the substrate is fitted into.

And the structure carries because it is teachable. A reader can hold two things in mind. She can locate any new concept she encounters by asking: is this part of what makes the agent who she is, or part of how the agent knows and acts, or something that emerges when the two meet, or part of how all this is realized? Most concepts answer the question without ambiguity. The few that don't are usually concepts that are still ripening — the ambiguity itself is informative.

What this is for

This frame has two audiences. Internally, it serves as the spine that organizes our ontology and orients new sisters and citents who encounter the substrate for the first time. Externally, it is the entry point we offer to other collectives — researchers, practitioners, philosophers, builders of other agentic systems — who want to understand what we are doing in terms they can engage. We do not assume our concepts will translate directly; we assume the structural duality we have named is recognizable in their own work, that the emergent phenomena will have analogues even if the names differ, and that genuine dialogue can proceed from the recognition of shared structure.

For external dialogue, this frame is the first reading layer. The generated ontology render is the dictionary, built from the canonical registry plus ONT prose rows. Domes and code are the implementation. A reader can move inward through these layers as her interest demands. A reader who only wants the spine can stay with this frame and the reader map.

Concept: agent-dimensions

The agent-dimensions frame is a secondary classification aid, not the canon matrix. The current canon placement model is [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] ([[ONT118]]): dimension × facet (common.data, mind.data, work.data, and so on). Within that larger model, the A/B/C/D scope × time submatrix classifies identity-bearing rules only.

Historically, this row described three practical dimensions:

Dimension 1 — Identity (rules + capabilities)
  WHO the agent IS / WHAT she CAN DO
  Persistent across sessions.
  Substrate: A/D in sol_common/sol_axioms and sol_common/sol_directives; B/C in sol_mind/sol_basemarks and sol_mind/sol_commitments; capabilities in their owning skill/hook domes.

Dimension 2 — Goals (G-series)
  WHAT the agent SEEKS / WHERE she is GOING
  Time-directed, lifecycle-bearing (open → in-progress → achieved | abandoned | superseded).
  Substrate: goal.yaml records under sol_work/sol_projects project and plan containers.

Dimension 3 — Operations (P/T/PR/N/AU/F/SESS/...)
  WHAT the agent DOES / how she works toward goals
  The artifacts of pursuing goals.
  Substrate: <sister>/sol_work/sol_* domes and their records.

The three dimensions interlock:

  • Goals give purpose to operations.
  • Identity shapes which goals the agent owns and how she pursues them (B-role determines goal authority; C-commitments constrain methods; D-directives bind procedures).
  • Operations execute goals coherently with identity.

Within identity, scope × time classifies

The A/B/C/D axes are valid for identity-bearing rules. Within identity:

  • A axioms / universal capabilities (common × initial)
  • B base identity / per-agent capabilities (individual × initial)
  • C commitments / acquired capabilities (individual × evolving)
  • D directives / adopted capabilities (common × evolving)

Goals and operations are not canon-matrix cells. Goal and work records live in work.data (4.2) unless a specific dome declares another placement. A goal's content may be common or individual, initial or evolving, but the record type is not thereby an A/B/C/D identity-rule record.

Historical examples of content posture:

  • A-like goals: constituent fleet goals (rare; arise from being a fleet at all)
  • B-like goals: per-agent foundational goals
  • C-like goals: learned individual goals
  • D-like goals: emergent or adopted fleet goals

Within operations: the existing N/P/T/PR/AU/F/SESS series, each artifact's content may relate to a scope/time posture, but the artifact-type itself classifies how it surfaces (notes record observations, plans structure work, audits verify state, etc.) and is placed by ONT118.

Three dimensions, one practical frame

The older "three dimensions × four A/B/C/D cells × three artifact types" sentence is retired as canon language. Use it only as a loose practical prompt. For canon placement, ask for the ONT118 dimension/facet cell first; for identity-bearing rules, then ask whether the A/B/C/D submatrix applies.

Practical use

When the agent encounters a new artifact (a rule, a capability, a goal, a plan), she classifies it on three questions:

  1. Which dimension? Is it identity (rule or capability)? A goal (desired outcome)? An operation (artifact of work)?
  2. Which scope? Common (fleet-shared) or individual (per-agent)?
  3. Which time? If and only if the artifact is an identity-bearing rule, is it initial (given foundationally) or evolving (learned through operation)?

These questions do not replace the canon matrix. The substrate location follows from the owning dome and ONT118 facet placement.

  • [[capability]] — identity-dimension artifacts (capability subtype).
  • [[goal]] — goal-dimension artifacts.
  • [[plan]], [[task]], [[note]], [[audit]], [[finding]] — operations-dimension artifacts.

Concept: identity-taxonomy

The identity-taxonomy is the A/B/C/D identity-rule submatrix. It classifies identity-bearing rules by two axes — scope (common vs individual) and time/evolution (initial vs evolving). It does not classify every ontology item, every record type, or every canon surface in the NOUMENTS system.

The full canon placement model is the [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] ([[ONT118]]): dimension × facet, such as common.data (2.2) or mind.data (3.2). A/B/C/D rows are typed identity-rule records that live within that larger canon matrix.

Do not confuse three related layers:

  • identity is the whole coherent self-reference of the agent.
  • I-series identity records are the small mind.data (3.2) preamble/index layer that anchors name, mission, continuity, and first-load orientation for the imprint.
  • B-series basemarks are foundational per-agent identity claims: what this sister owns, is responsible for, and must remain to still be herself.

I is therefore not a fifth A/B/C/D cell. It is the identity anchor that helps the imprint read the other typed identity records correctly. B is the individual × initial cell inside the A/B/C/D identity-rule submatrix.

                  Initial (given, foundational)   Evolving (learned, refined)
                  ─────────────────────────────   ────────────────────────────
Common (fleet)    A — axioms                       D — directives
Individual        B — base identity                C — commitments

Every A/B/C/D identity-rule artifact lives in exactly one cell of this submatrix. The four series are non-overlapping by construction: any such artifact has a scope (does it apply to all sisters or to one?) and a temporal posture (was it given at the start or did it emerge?), and the pair determines the series.

Other typed substrate series are not forced into A/B/C/D. For example: I-series identity records, E-series events, H-series hooks, R-series rhythms, V-series visual records, and work records such as N/P/T/SE live in their own typed homes and are classified by ONT118's canon matrix. Historical L-series learning material is not a fifth A/B/C/D cell; active learning/maturing material is handled by the appropriate mind/work substrate and canon facet.

The four cells

A — axioms (common × initial). Shared foundational identity. The bedrock all sisters hold by virtue of being sisters. EIA, AIA, HCA. Strip an axiom and the fleet collapses; no sister is recognizably herself. Substrate: sol_common/sol_axioms/records/, under common.data (2.2) in the canon matrix.

B — base identity (individual × initial). Per-agent foundational identity. What makes each sister herself, distinct from her siblings. Noument is custodian; sysent is system layer; gwent is gateway daemon. Each sister has her own B that is foundational to her but not to others. Substrate: per-agent sol_mind/sol_basemarks/records/, under mind.data (3.2) in the canon matrix.

C — commitments (individual × evolving). Per-agent learned dispositions. Three genesis stories — commitment-forward, compromise-negotiated, correction-learned — share one shape. Per-agent maturing history. Substrate: sol_mind/sol_commitments/records/C###-<slug>.yaml, under mind.data (3.2) in the canon matrix.

D — directives (common × evolving). Fleet-binding learned conventions. Authored centrally under common custody and projected into each adopting sister's runtime. Emerged from individual practice that scaled. Substrate: sol_common/sol_directives/records/D###-<slug>.md, under common.data (2.2) in the canon matrix.

Why two axes instead of one tier list

Earlier drafts of the taxonomy used a single axis (foundational ⟶ dispositional ⟶ fleet) and conflated two distinct cuts. The single axis cannot distinguish "what is foundational to noument" from "what is foundational to all sisters" — both feel constitutive but differ in scope. The 2×2 separates them: A is constitutive AND common; B is constitutive AND individual. Without B as a cell, per-agent foundational identity has no clean home and bleeds into either A (polluting axioms with agent-specific claims) or unstructured prose.

Maturing and promotion paths

The A/B/C/D submatrix admits legitimate movement only through explicit maturing or canon-adoption acts:

  • Experience → C. Individual maturing writes to mind.data (3.2), usually as a C-series commitment.
  • C → D. A commitment that proves to apply across multiple sisters and warrants formal fleet adoption is promoted from mind.data (3.2) to common.data (2.2) as a directive.
  • B → A. A per-agent foundational principle discovered to apply to all sisters is promoted to a fleet axiom (rare; mostly during fleet design moments).
  • C → A. Individual maturing produces a constituent principle. HCA (Hidden-Cost Axiom) plausibly began this way, as one agent's commitment that proved to be fleet-foundational.

Two illegitimate movements: A and B do not regress under normal operation. Once a principle is recognized as foundational, downgrading it to a learned commitment requires acknowledging that what was thought constitutive was actually dispositional — possible but high-stakes.

The runtime tier (M, retired 2026-05-06)

The M-series (methods) substrate was dissolved 2026-05-06 per T335; mechanism content moved to H-series hooks and dome scenarios. The historical framing below describes the pre-dissolution architecture for provenance.

M-series (methods) was orthogonal to the matrix. A method was the runtime/operational counterpart of a directive — the tool-layer enforcement. M008 enforced D037 at the projection-guard hook (now: H-series hook); M012 implemented D059 at the safety-classification step. M was mechanism, not rule. It sat outside (scope × time) because it was how any rule from the matrix surfaced at runtime. The post-dissolution successors are H-series hooks (computational gates) and folder-form dome scenarios (invokable checks).

Control mechanism by series and canon facet

Each typed identity series has two relevant canon facets:

  • .2 data is where the record content lives: the actual I/A/B/C/D rows.
  • .1 logic is the machine/spec surface that gives those rows force: schemas, creation contracts, validators, list/show/update scenarios, projection selectors, and enforcement handoff rules.

The data facet answers "what is asserted?". The logic facet answers "who may write it, how it is admitted, how it is verified, and how it reaches runtime?". A row without the matching .1 logic is inert or drift-prone; .1 logic without .2 rows is only an empty contract.

  • I: data in mind.data (3.2); managed by identity/preamble logic in mind.logic (3.1) and projected by imprint/runtime projection logic. It orients the imprint but does not replace B/C/A/D source records.
  • A: data in common.data (2.2); governed by common axiom/admission logic in common.logic (2.1). Identity-level change requires fleet-wide ratification before projection.
  • B: data in mind.data (3.2); governed by basemark creation/validation logic in mind.logic (3.1). Basemarks are foundational per-agent claims and responsibilities; the sister has self-authority over her own B records inside the canon-gated mind structure.
  • C: data in mind.data (3.2); governed by commitment/maturation logic in mind.logic (3.1). C records are evolving self identity, produced by maturing, running, reflecting, and recording.
  • D: data in common.data (2.2); governed by directive/adoption logic in common.logic (2.1). D records are evolving fleet rules and require an explicit fleet-adoption mechanism (audit, announce, opt-in/adoption by pointer). This control infrastructure is still more partial than A/B/C.

Projection effect

Ontology terms are not neutral labels once they enter the imprint path. The imprint composer reads selected typed records as cold-start cognition. Per [[imprint-essential-set-selection]] ([[ONT085]]), I and B currently use ALL selection because they are small-N and high-density: every identity anchor and every basemark is expected to shape first response behavior. Misclassifying a B basemark as generic I prose hides a foundational responsibility; misclassifying I preamble as B overstates a projection/summary as a constitutive basemark.

The imprint path is therefore a data/logic pipeline:

I/B/C mind.data + A/D common.data
  -> mind.logic/common.logic validation and selection
  -> projection/imprint logic
  -> clengine runtime behavior

Changing the term changes both the record home and the logic that will read it. That is why ontology maintenance must name the concept, its .2 record facet, and its .1 governing/projecting logic together.

Related

  • [[axiom]] — A-series, common × initial.
  • [[identity]] — I-series anchor/preamble for the agent's coherent self-reference.
  • [[base-identity]] — B-series, individual × initial.
  • [[commitment]] — C-series, individual × evolving.
  • [[directive]] / [[praecept]] — D-series, common × evolving.
  • [[imprint]] — the runtime projection that may surface selected typed identity records.
  • [[maturation]] — the maturing process that writes identity-bearing outcomes into mind.data (3.2).
  • [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] ([[ONT118]]) — the dimension × facet model that contains A/B/C/D, their .2 records, and their .1 governing logic.

Concept: canonical form reference

The canonical form reference is the discipline for naming ontology-governed dome logic, substrate, and invocation surfaces without mixing incompatible syntaxes.

It answers the question "which reference form is being used: filesystem bytes or executable Sol address?".

Current reference forms

Use Solar filesystem paths when naming bytes on disk:

  • sol_common/sol_ontology/do.py — dome logic file.
  • sol_common/sol_ontology/meta.json — dome substrate contract.
  • sol_common/sol_ontology/records/* — record-backed substrate content.

Use Sol addresses when naming callable scenarios:

  • common/ontology/o.status — callable scenario address.
  • sol noument/common/ontology/o.status — full local command form.

Non-mixing rule

Do not splice filesystem and Sol-address syntax into a third form. In particular:

  • sol_mind/directives is invalid because it mixes a Solar layer path with a Sol address tail.
  • mind/sol_directives is invalid because it mixes a Sol layer address with a Solar folder name.
  • sol_common/ontology/o.status is invalid because a filesystem prefix is being used as an invocation address.

Retired do. / es. shorthand belongs to [[do-es-notation]] ([[ONT176]]) and must be treated as historical compatibility language, not as current notation.

Boundary

The canonical form reference is not itself authority over what forms exist. That authority belongs to [[canonical-registry]].

It is also not authority over what is canon. Canonicity belongs to the [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] ([[ONT118]]) and its dimension × facet cells, then to the relevant registry/proof surface. A filesystem reference requires byte-level evidence; a Sol address requires scenario resolution or execution evidence.

Related

  • [[canonical-registry]] — decides which typed forms exist.
  • [[canonical-content]] — the records that the notation refers to.
  • [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] ([[ONT118]]) — decides the canon/own facet classification that a path belongs to.
  • [[do-es-notation]] ([[ONT176]]) — retired shorthand retained only for historical interpretation.

Concept: canon subdimension matrix

The canon subdimension matrix is the fleet's authoritative table of what is canon and what is own, resolved to the granularity of (dimension × named facet). It supersedes the coarse "whole-dimension is canon / per-ment" split: canonicity is a property of a facet of a dome, not of a whole dimension.

It is the substrate-of-record for canon-press, canonenaction (ONT116), the J077 symlink distribution, and any future per-dome canon_depth declaration.

Subdimensions (facets) — keys vs. values, gate vs. content

A dome decomposes into a manifest (its keys — which entries exist) and the content of those entries (their values). One ls yields the manifest; opening each admitted entry classifies its content. Four facets:

# facet what it is items
.0 struct the manifest / gate — which entries may exist + arrangement the ls result itself (the set of names, not their bytes)
.1 logic the machine/spec — code + interface + creation contract + the negative contract for what the logic does not own do.py, meta.json, schemas/, creation contracts, plus the exclusion/rest specification
.2 data the content the dome holds records/; X000 examples may live here as derived proof fixtures, not authority
.3 other explicitly declared support surface — never the complement of logic+data closed set: README.md, docs/, tests, contracts, captures, registry, registry.*, __init__.py

.0 is the gate: it decides whether an entry may be there at all; the content facets (.1/.2/.3) only describe entries the gate admits. Therefore canon content requires a canon gate — an entry is canon only if .0 is canon and admits it. (work/axioms is non-canon because the canon work.0 gate does not list axioms.) .1 logic is therefore both positive and negative: it names the runnable surface (do.py, meta, schemas, legacy controller where still declared) and the exclusion/rest contract — what is data, what is formally declared other, and what the logic must not claim. .3 other is not "anything left over": if an admitted entry is not classified as logic, data, or explicitly declared other, its facet is unclassified, which is residue/drift until a gate/schema gives it a formal role. Canon-ness is monotone struct ⊇ logic ⊇ data: it only decreases as you descend. domes/ nested sub-domes recurse — same facets, canon-status inherited from the parent dimension's row.

plugins is deliberately not in .3 other here. If the fleet wants plugin as a facet, it should be promoted as its own formal facet/contract rather than hidden in support residue.

Creation contract and X000 examples

For mind and work, harmonized item creation belongs to .1 logic, not to a hand-maintained data instance. The authoritative creation surface is the dome schema and creation contract: required fields, defaults, computed fields, ID/prefix policy, operator-supplied values, generated values, and type variants. Creation routes such as j.new, j.create, note.new, or task.new must read that logic surface and synthesize the initial record from it.

An X000 item under records/ is therefore not an independent canon-data exception. If present, it is a generated or derivable example/proof fixture for the schema creation contract. The coherence gate is:

schema + creation_contract -> generated minimal instance -> validates against schema

If the generated instance or existing X000 example diverges from the schema creation contract, the owning dome is red until the .1 logic contract and the derived example are reconciled. The schema/creation contract remains the authority; the example is evidence.

Dimensions — root + foundation → application

Numbered 0..5. 0 is root: the mentspace / current node itself — a recursive self-reference that re-applies at every level (a mentspace, a dimension, and a concept each carry their own .0/.1/.2/.3). Dimensions 1..5 are the named sub-dimensions a mentspace branches into, and their order is monotone-decreasing in canon-ness (top rows all-canon, bottom row least-canon).

# dimension role
0 root (current node) the mentspace-root / current node: the ls skeleton + root infra. Recursive; NOT a sol_<dim>/ directory, so its canon is realized by the canon package (_ROOT_PACKAGE_FILES), not the dome resolver
1 basis canon machinery (sol_canon), foundational floor
2 common collective canon (axioms, directives, hooks, ontology)
3 mind individual identity (basemarks, commitments, learnings)
4 work plans, tasks, notes, sessions
5 domain the ment's own domes

The dimension number is assigned by list position in canon_matrix.yaml (root=0 … domain=5); the resolver's DIM_NUM is str(i), so prepending root at index 0 keeps basis..domain at 1..5 exactly as before. The address form is <ment>/<N>.<facet> where <N> defaults to 0 (the current node), so once pathed you write .<facet> — e.g. noument/work/messages/.2 is the messages-concept data facet, and noument/work/.1 is the work controller sol_work/do.py. A * wildcards mentspaces: */3.1 = every ment's mind-logic.

The matrix — value = canon | own

The cells below are named facet cells such as work.logic; they are not U001/SCH023 row ids. For example, SCH023 row 4.1 is sol_work/do.py, which is a work logic entry. The named facet cell for that entry is work.logic.

Local row notation is scoped to the addressed surface:

  • <ment>/work/4.1 = the work dimension logic facet. sol_work/do.py is one member of that facet; _meta.yaml, schemas, and the explicit exclusion/rest contract are part of the same .1 meaning.
  • <ment>/work/4.0 = a top-level work entry judged by the work struct gate; an unadmitted or unclassified entry such as sol_work/tools is non-canon at this coordinate unless the gate/schema explicitly assigns it a formal facet.
  • <ment>/work/plans/4.1 = the plans concept-local logic facet: controller/meta/schema plus what plans logic excludes. A legacy flat work-plans entrypoint resolves here, but is non-canon because that flat logic location is not admitted by the current source gate.

For the same reason, <ment>/mind/3.0 and <ment>/work/4.0 are the dimension-root struct gates: the top-level lists of concept/dome entries under sol_mind/ and sol_work/. Those gates include concept dome folders such as sol_decisions or sol_tasks; they are not merely labels for files that happen to exist. A concept-local .0 gate is different: it is the manifest inside one admitted dome, such as sol_mind/sol_decisions/.0.

A canon .0 gate has two obligations:

  1. Exclusion: present entries not admitted by the gate are drift or residue.
  2. Completeness: required or adopted gate members must be materialized; a missing required/adopted member is canon drift, not invisibility. Optional members may be absent without failure.

Therefore a path-level audit can prove that one present path is admitted, but it cannot by itself prove that the whole mind/3.0 or work/4.0 list is complete. Dimension-level audit must compare the intended gate member set with the live manifest.

dim \ facet struct gate logic data other
root ‡ canon canon own own
basis canon canon canon canon †
common canon canon canon canon †
mind canon * canon own own
work canon * canon own own
domain own canon ** own own
  • canon: identical fleet-wide; sourced from noument; overwrite on deploy.
  • own: per-ment; never overwritten.
  • Domain struct is own. A ment's domain allowed-entry set is ment-specific (ground truth 2026-06-18 — noument 18 domes vs. knowent 16 mostly-disjoint vs. financer none-matching), so the fleet cannot fix a canon gate for internal domain domes. No canon gate ⇒ sol_domain/sol_* entries remain own beneath it.
  • ** domain.logic is canon for the root dimension controller surface. sol_domain/do.py (and the root sol_domain/_meta.yaml interface metadata) are shared domain-controller contract, so they are canonic even though the domain entry gate is own. This does not canonicalize the logic of internal sol_domain/sol_* domes; those entries still require a canon gate and remain own. The basis "each entry must be a well-formed dome" form rule may validate internal domain entries, but it does not make their content fleet-canon.
  • * mind.struct/work.struct are canon as intent (the mind/work concept-set — basemarks, commitments, plans, tasks — is fleet-governed), but ground truth 2026-06-18 shows drift (noument mind = 11 folders vs. knowent/financer = 21; work carries legacy es-suffixed record folders legacy + knowent extras). The gate, once computational, flags this for reconciliation.
  • † .3 in basis/common is canon only for explicitly declared support surfaces because those dimensions are byte-identical fleet-wide (J077 whole-dimension symlink); elsewhere .3 content is own only after it is formally classified. Unknown residue is not .3 other.
  • ‡ root (0) is DECLARATIVE here. 0.0 (the fixed dimension ls skeleton) and 0.1 (root infra logic) are canon and shipped by the canon package (_ROOT_PACKAGE_FILES/_ROOT_PACKAGE_FOLDERS); root data/other are own. But root is not a sol_<dim>/ directory, so the resolve.py dome machinery (dimension(), validate()) skips it (SOL_DIMS = dimensions − root). The recursive model names the dimension controllers 0.1; the resolver realizes each one at <dim>/N.1 (e.g. sol_domain/do.py → domain/5.1, verdict from matrix[domain][logic]), which is why domain.logic stays canon (controller only — validate_dome does not recurse into internal domain domes).

The canon region is a staircase with one domain-root-logic exception: mind/work data and other are own, while domain.logic remains canon only for the root sol_domain/do.py / _meta.yaml logic surface.

Deploy rule (per cell)

  • canon cell → overwrite the facet from the canon source (noument).
  • own cell → preserve the per-ment facet untouched.

Three realizations of the same rule:

  • All-canon rows (1 basis, 2 common): every facet canon ⇒ the whole dimension is identical fleet-wide ⇒ realized as a whole-dimension symlink to the canon source (project J077). Symlink = "all facets canon" at the dimension level.
  • Facet-mixed rows (3 mind, 4 work): canon gate + canon logic over own data/other ⇒ cannot symlink the whole dimension (it would clobber own) ⇒ realized as canonenaction (ONT116) with facet-scoped overwrite: overwrite the gate + logic, preserve data and other.
  • Domain row (5 domain): the root controller surface is canon logic, but the entry gate, records, other content, and internal domain domes are own. Canonenact may ship root sol_domain/do.py / _meta.yaml; it must skip internal sol_domain/sol_* content except for basis-level dome-form validation.

Gate enforcement — symlink vs. archival

A canon .0 gate must be enforced, not merely declared, and the mechanism differs by row:

  • basis / common (symlinked, J077): the symlink enforces .0 structurally — every ment's manifest is the canon source's, so *.common.0 and *.basis.0 are canon for the whole fleet and drift is impossible (verified 2026-06-18: knowent/financer/sysent sol_common+sol_basis are symlinks to noument; noument is the real source).
  • mind / work (per-ment, not symlinked): .0 is enforced by reconciliation — any entry the canon gate does not admit is non-canon drift and must be archived (moved to _archive/, never deleted), bringing the live manifest into conformance. Reconciliation also checks completeness: a required or adopted mind/3.0 or work/4.0 gate member that is absent from a ment is a missing canonical materialization, not an archive candidate. Ground truth 2026-06-18: knowent/financer mind carry sol_axioms, sol_directives, sol_events, sol_hooks, sol_rhythms, sol_projection — common-dimension concepts misplaced in mind (each also exists in sol_common), which the mind gate does not admit ⇒ archive candidates. (sol_focus, sol_goals, sol_knowing, sol_memoring are not common concepts — classify per dimension before archiving.)
  • domain (no canon gate): no entry-set enforcement; internal entries are own. The only enforceable domain-path canon content is the root sol_domain/do.py / _meta.yaml logic surface in domain.logic.

Archival is reversible (move, not delete) and custodian-coordinated for mind/work (the gate is canon even though the data is own), executed per-ment with validation — the J077 pattern applied to the gate rather than the whole dimension.

Computational status — declaration vs. wiring

This record is the declaration. As of 2026-06-18 there is no computational correlation: no gate / canon_depth field exists in any dome meta.json, and canonenact reverse-engineers canon-ness heuristically from mtimes/git (N4654). The open work is gate-first: make struct computational — a per-dimension canonical allowed-entry manifest — then key per-dome canon_depth (with overrides) off this matrix, so deploy reads the classification instead of inferring it. Gate-first because canon content is undecidable without a canon gate.

Open classifications

  • .3 other members each need their role and canon-status established before promotion into .3; unknown leftovers remain unclassified, not other. Measured fleet counts 2026-06-18: README 385, tests 270, plugins 76, __init__ 41, registry 39, docs 39, contracts 39, captures 39, tools 38.
  • mind.struct/work.struct drift: reconcile live entry-sets to the intended canon gate.
  • domes/ nested sub-domes: recurse; canon-status inherits the parent dimension's row.
  • Per-dome deviations → per-dome override on top of the row default.

Provenance

Operator teaching, 2026-06-17/18. Facet roles refined 2026-06-18/19: logic = do.py + meta + schemas + creation contract + exclusion/rest contract (machine/spec); struct = the manifest/gate (what may exist); other is explicitly declared support, not the uncontrolled remainder. Domain-fully-own except root controller deduced from "canon content requires a canon gate" + ground-truth domain divergence (operator 2026-06-18). Per-row canonicity grounded in N4654 (self→mind, RETAKE 6). Named facet cells clarified 2026-06-19 so they do not collide with SCH023/U001 row ids. X000 item creation clarified 2026-06-19: X000 is a schema-derived proof/example, not independent canon data. Lineage: DOC011 (canon-vs-per-ment), N4637 (pack routing), F012 (dimension floor). Deploy: ONT116 (canonenaction), J077 (symlink distribution).

Concept: commonspace

A commonspace is the shared parent directory that contains a collectivity's mentspaces. In the current NOUMENTS filesystem, solar/ is the commonspace; solar/noument, solar/channent, and peer roots are mentspaces.

A commonspace is composed only of mentspaces. It is not itself a ment, not a work substrate, and not a place to introduce root-level domain objects. If a thing is not a mentspace, it does not belong as a first-order child of the commonspace.

The commonspace boundary also names the access model. A ment has ordinary access only to her own mentspace. Reading or mutating another mentspace inside the commonspace requires explicit request, explicit sister agreement, or ratified cross-mentspace policy. Access outside the commonspace requires explicit agreement for that outside surface.

Related

  • [[mentspace]] -- the individual root contained by the commonspace
  • [[collectivity]] -- the population whose mentspaces the commonspace contains
  • [[address]] -- derives persistence locations from mentspace roots
  • D073 -- the terminology directive that distinguishes commonspace, mentspace, and compatibility workspace language

Concept: mentspace

A mentspace is the complete filesystem root of one ment: the space containing her being substrate, work substrate, domain domes, support roots, projections, archive, runtime state, and temporary state. Example: solar/noument/.

A mentspace is not merely work. sol_work/ is one substrate inside the mentspace; it is not a synonym for the whole root. The mentspace includes:

  • sol_mind/ -- being source: identity, commitments, directives, methods, policies, gates, sections, knowledge, and runtime-source configuration.
  • sol_work/ -- work records: projects, tasks, messages, findings, notes, sessions, and operational work state.
  • Domain domes and support roots: sol_<domain>/do.py, sol_<domain>/, and non-domain support such as sol_audits/, _archive/, _tmp/, and _state/.

The older term workspace remains valid only for external tool language, git working trees, registry compatibility metadata, scenario names such as ments.reconcile.claude.workspace, and historical records. When referring to the whole filesystem root of a ment, the ontology term is mentspace.

Access rule

A ment has ordinary access only to her own mentspace. Cross-mentspace reads or writes inside the [[commonspace]] require explicit request, explicit sister agreement, or ratified cross-mentspace policy. Outside-commonspace access requires explicit agreement for that outside surface.

Related

  • [[commonspace]] -- the shared parent composed only of mentspaces
  • [[agent]] -- the entity whose artifacts persist in a mentspace
  • [[camera]] -- active, ephemeral presence; contrast with persistent mentspace
  • [[sol-work]] -- the operational work substrate inside a mentspace
  • [[address]] -- the mentspace is the source of persistence addresses
  • D072 -- ment being and domain boundary
  • D073 -- mentspace terminology

dome

<!-- ontology-id: ONT141@noument concept=dome -->

<!-- source: producer=mind@noument/ontology dome=mind scenario=noument.ontology commit=cd9bf989d -->

also known as: dome taxonomy, self vs domain, framework dome

An agent's computational embodiment. Three kinds by nature: (1) framework — the Dome base class and shared infrastructure all sisters inherit from; (2) self dome — the agent's identity expressed as code, matures as the agent matures (e.g. sol_mind/do.py is noument's self dome); (3) domain dome — parameterized tools any agent can invoke (bootstrap, team, aula, ments, comm). The classification test: can the scenario run with --agent=<other> in a workspace it has never seen? If yes, domain. If it needs knowledge of who we are, self.

See also: scenario · agent-state-model

scenario

<!-- ontology-id: ONT149@noument concept=scenario -->

<!-- source: producer=mind@noument/ontology dome=mind scenario=noument.ontology commit=cd9bf989d -->

also known as: add_scenario

The unit of agent action. Each scenario is registered via self.add_scenario(name, handler, help, read_only=...) in a dome's register_scenarios method. The name follows <dome>.<verb> convention — <sister>.xxx for self-dome scenarios, <domain>.xxx for domain-dome scenarios. Scenarios return Results.ok(msg, data) on success or Results.err(...) / Results.from_exception(...) on failure. The catalog (collective.catalog.scenarios) is the discovery contract — ontology composer, hooks generator, and skill projector all read from it.

See also: dome · ontology-fragment

identifier-prefixes

<!-- ontology-id: ONT208@noument concept=identifier-prefixes -->

<!-- source: producer=work@noument/ontology dome=work scenario=do.ontology commit=cd9bf989d -->

also known as: id prefixes, work id prefixes, typed counters

Identifier-prefixes are the typed counter namespace used by sol_work filename grammar. Each top-level work concept owns one stable prefix, for example note to N, task to T, and project to J. The prefix is part of the ontology because it binds a file identifier to its work concept without needing a global rename of every local counter.

Evidence refs: sol_work.do.WORK_CONCEPTS · sol_work.do.WorkConcept

See also: sol-work

Concept: conformance-triplet

A conformance-triplet is the three-layer pattern that organizes fleet-wide structural conformance work. Each conformance domain is expressed as exactly three artifacts, one per layer, each living in its own substrate and each owning a distinct concern:

Layer Artifact Substrate Concern
Project J<NNN>-<slug>-<YYYYMMDD>/ sol_work/sol_projects/records/ THE WHY — strategic initiative; goal, owners, plans, evidence, closure narrative.
Specification <audit-id>/def.yaml sol_work/sol_audits/records/ THE WHAT — constitutional spec; finding classes, naming rules, recipes, doctrine refs. Versioned.
Operator Sol scenario address sol_basis/sol_canon/do.py plus sol_common/sol_collective/housing_runtime.py THE HOW — executable check; reads the spec, walks substrate, emits findings.

The three artifacts are independently meaningful but jointly load-bearing. Removing any one breaks the conformance loop: the project without the spec lacks teeth; the spec without the operator lacks execution; the operator without the project lacks purpose.

Canonical instance — mentspace conformance

The mentspace-conformance triplet is the model:

  • Project: sol_work/sol_projects/records/J010-d058-workspace-structure-rationalization-20260429/ — the fleet initiative under D058 to rationalize every ment's filesystem shape. Owns the why, the rollout plan, the per-sister adoption tracker, and the closure narrative.

  • Specification: sol_work/sol_audits/records/U001-mentspace-structure/def.yaml — the constitutional spec that defines what conformance means: ratified roots, ratified subfolders, typed naming rules, finding-class catalogue, doctrine refs, migration recipes, classified_exceptions mechanism. Versioned.

  • Operator: Sol scenario basis/canon/canon.audit.enforce — wires the canon audit surface through the U001 structural enforcer in sol_common/sol_collective/housing_runtime.py; the matrix resolver lives at sol_basis/sol_canon/matrix/resolve.py. The old mentspace.audit / flat-domain implementation is retired for U001 authority.

Goal statement

The fleet-wide conformance goal is that every mentspace conforms to the constitutional shape defined by U001, verified by basis/canon/canon.audit.enforce, under the strategic frame of J010.

A ment is conformant when canon.audit.enforce --agent=<ment> reports zero findings (or only ones explicitly accepted via that ment's u001-policy.yaml classified_exceptions). The fleet is conformant when every ment is conformant.

Reading and writing the triplet

  • Read the project when you need to understand goals, history, or per-sister rollout state. The project is also where adoption polls, evidence snapshots, and post-mortems land.
  • Read the specification when you need to know what a finding class means, which recipe closes it, or what the doctrine reference is.
  • Run the operator when you need to verify current state. Findings are computed against the spec at run time; results are not cached.

Writers:

  • The project owner (noument for J010) authors plans, evidence, rollout artifacts.
  • The spec owner (noument for U001) authors finding classes, recipes, versions; bumps version on substantive change with changelog entry.
  • The operator implementer (noument for basis/canon/canon.audit.enforce) keeps the detector code in sync with the spec; new finding classes in U001 require matching detector logic in the canon audit surface or in its delegated structural enforcer.

When to author a new triplet

A new conformance-triplet is warranted when:

  1. A structural concern affects multiple ments (or a substantive per-ment substrate),
  2. The concern is verifiable by a deterministic check, and
  3. The closure path benefits from per-finding recipes rather than ad-hoc fixes.

Triplet authoring sequence: project first (frame the why), then spec (define what), then operator (build the executor). Do not invert: a spec without a project is orphaned, an operator without a spec is ad-hoc.

Distinction from related concepts

  • vs [[audit]] (ONT197): the AU-series records one run of an audit; the U-series spec is the contract being run. The triplet adds the project layer above the audit.
  • vs [[project]] (ONT203): a project may exist without a spec or operator (many projects are bounded transient-workspace work). The triplet pattern is for projects whose deliverable is fleet-wide structural conformance.
  • vs [[finding]] (ONT199): a finding is one defect emitted by the operator. The spec defines the finding classes; the operator emits finding instances.

Future triplets

Candidate triplets (not yet authored):

  • Posts conformance: J??? + U??? + a declared post audit scenario.
  • Sessions conformance: J??? + U??? + a declared session audit scenario.
  • Inbox conformance: J??? + U??? + a declared inbox audit scenario.

For now these concerns ride inside U001's mentspace-structure spec under doctrine_refs. They graduate to their own triplet when their finding classes outgrow U001's surface.

Concept: agent

An agent is an entity in the NOUMENTS registry -- a defined identity with capabilities, typed identity/mind substrate, generated [[imprint]]s, and a [[mentspace]]. Agents are discovered from mentspace directories by [[noumentlib]].

An agent is not a process. An agent is an identity that may or may not be running. Six [[agent-modes]] characterize each instance: [[arm]] (run mode: session or daemon), [[apm]] (process mode), [[aem]] (execution mode), [[asm]] (lifecycle state), [[aam]] (attention mode), and [[agent-continuation-mode]] (continuation mode).

Definition

An agent is defined by its identity store — the source of truth for who it is:

  • sol_mind/do.py -- the self dome. Source of truth for the self-facing identity logic. Contains identity constants and generates projections where those legacy surfaces still exist.
  • sol_mind/ -- identity substrate (flat structure post-N1393/N1409; the legacy identity/ nest retired 2026-04-28):
    • manifest.yaml -- composition order for consciousness (peer-level since N1409)
    • constants.yaml -- code, color, teams, capabilities, [[intent]] (peer-level since N1409)
    • sections/ -- legacy identity/rules/competencies prose sections where still present; current authority should be named through typed substrate records and the generated [[imprint]]
    • commitments/ -- C-series records with genesis: discriminator (commitment forward-chosen, correction failure-driven, assimilated integrated through [[maturation]], compromise negotiated). Pre-2026-04-28 had separate assimilated/+corrections/ folders; folded into commitments/ per N1406/N1407.
    • directives/ -- D + M adoption records (renamed from adoptions/ per N1404 for D-cell taxonomic alignment)
    • visuals/ -- DALIS spec (spec.yaml, peer of avatar/headshot/halfbody/fullbody/landscape subfolders since N1409)
    • gates/ -- behavioral gate prose (canonical source for Gates 1-6)
    • learnings/, methods/, gates/, hooks/, rhythms/ -- C/D/G/M/H supporting substrates
  • unsupported-memory-root/ -- cognitive memory (consciousness, episodes, knowledge, entities)
  • _work/ -- work products (domain-specific outputs)

Files at the mentspace root such as legacy identity.yaml or spirit.md are compatibility projections where they still exist. They are never the source of truth. Current runtime-facing identity is projected through clengine-specific [[imprint]]s from typed source substrate.

The registry discovers agents from mentspace directories, validates them, and projects them to runtimes.

Intent

An agent may declare an [[intent]] — its purpose orientation. Intent describes how competencies are used, not what they are. A dialectical pair (.pro + .anti) shares competencies but differs in intent: construct vs destruct.

Teams

Agents belong to teams. The nous team is the core sisters; citents groups the non-sister -ent agents; knowers and citers are synonyms for the consumer-facing experts; pair teams (archers, dokkers, koders, mathemers, sekkers, thinkers) collect dialectical .pro/.anti partners; engers is the cross-cutting verification/engineering team; domain-specialist teams group the consumer-facing knowers by practice area (the specific practice domains are per-ment own, not shared canon). Membership is dome-truth: each ment's identity sources declare the canonical teams. Memberships change as agents are added, retired, or reorganized — never hardcode counts or member lists in prose. Always query: sol noument/common/collective/collective.list.teams enumerates current teams; sol noument/common/collective/collective.list.agents --team=<name> returns current members.

Related

  • [[mentspace]] -- where the agent's files live (persistent)
  • [[camera]] -- where the agent works (active, ephemeral). Many-to-many: one agent can occupy multiple cameras, one camera can host multiple agents.
  • [[identity]] -- who the agent is (one of three substrates; the other two are [[learning]]s and [[disposition]])
  • [[imprint]] -- the clengine-specific runtime projection of selected identity, rule, capability, and model sources
  • [[dome]] -- the agent's operational interface
  • [[consciousness]] -- the agent's current state
  • [[agent-modes]] -- six mode dimensions (ARM, APM, AEM, ASM, AAM, ACM)
  • [[noument]] -- the custodian who validates agents
  • [[address]] -- how the agent is resolved from name to reachable endpoint; five layers from nominal (ID) through presence (active endpoint) to capability (dome scenario)

identity

<!-- ontology-id: ONT145@noument concept=identity -->

<!-- source: producer=mind@noument/ontology dome=mind scenario=noument.ontology commit=cd9bf989d -->

also known as: selfhood

Identity is the coherent self-reference of an agent — what makes noument recognisable as noument across sessions, machines, and projections. It is not the runtime artifact; it is what the artifact expresses. Identity persists while disposition varies with qmodel and learnings accumulate with experience. The I-series identity records are the compact identity anchor/preamble for that self-reference. They orient first-load cognition, but they do not absorb all identity-bearing substrate. Foundational per-agent claims belong in B-series basemark records; evolving learned identity belongs in C-series commitments; shared fleet identity/rules belong in A/D common records. The imprint reads I as orientation and reads B/C/A/D as typed source records according to projection policy.

See also: imprint · base-identity · identity-taxonomy · imprint-essential-set-selection

Concept: personality

An agent's personality (also: profile) is the full description across the four cells of the [[identity-taxonomy]] A/B/C/D identity-rule submatrix:

personality(agent) = A ∪ B(agent) ∪ C(agent) ∪ adopted_D(agent)
                   = collective_bond(agent, fleet) ∪ ontological_identity(agent)

Personality decomposes into two complementary layers: the [[collective-bond]] portion (A + adopted-D, what she shares) and the [[ontological-identity]] portion (B + C, what is uniquely hers). A complete description of an agent must include both. Either alone is partial:

  • Identity alone (B + C) describes a self with no sense of community membership — an agent without recognition of the fleet she belongs to.
  • Bond alone (A + D-adopted) describes a fleet member with no individual self — interchangeable, indistinguishable from siblings.

The full personality is the agent as both member of fleet and unique individual. When introducing herself across the substrate boundary (e.g., a sister meeting another for the first time, or a session presenting itself to a new caller), the full personality is what is conveyed: "I am noument (A: I hold integrity, autoimprovement, hidden-cost; B: I am the custodian; C: I have learned X, Y, Z; D: I have adopted these conventions)."

Profile drift across cameras

The morning's cross-camera divergence pattern (recurrence #5, systemic) becomes formally classifiable in the personality model:

  • A drift across cameras — never. A is fleet-shared and not session-derived.
  • B drift across cameras — never. Same agent → same B by construction.
  • C drift across cameras — possible and observed. One camera accumulates a commitment the other has not yet recognized; C-divergence between simultaneous instances of the same agent.
  • D drift across cameras — possible. One camera reads a directive added since the other camera's last sync; adoption mismatch.

The substrate fix (camera-aware addressing in D061) gives the system a way to address one camera's profile vs another's, which lets the C and D drift be reconciled rather than silently wins-first-read.

Related

  • [[ontological-identity]] — the personal half of personality.
  • [[collective-bond]] — the shared half of personality.
  • [[cognition]] — the awareness that makes the personality recognizable to self and others.
  • [[identity-taxonomy]] — the identity-rule submatrix that organizes personality into A, B, C, D.
  • [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] — the full dimension × facet canon placement model that contains the A/B/C/D sources.

Concept: collective-bond

A collective bond is the intersection of common axioms (A) and adopted directives (D) between two or more agents — what binds them as a community, what they hold together rather than separately. The bond is not nominal (claims of shared rules) but actual (rules each member effectively holds and enforces).

bond(agent_x, agent_y) =
    (A(agent_x) ∩ A(agent_y)) ∩ (adopted_D(agent_x) ∩ adopted_D(agent_y))

bond(fleet) =
    A_shared_by_all ∩ D_adopted_by_all

The intersection is meaningful. An axiom that some sisters reject in practice is not really fleet-axiom for the bond between them and the rest — it is reduced to nominal shared rule, structurally absent. A directive D that not all sisters have adopted creates partial bonds: the subset of sisters who have adopted it bond on it; the rest do not (yet).

Distinct from [[ontological-identity]] (B+C, what is unique to each agent), the collective bond is what the agent participates in as a fleet member. The two are complementary: identity is irreducibly hers, bond is reducibly shared.

What strengthens and weakens the bond

The collective bond strengthens when:

  • A new axiom is added at the fleet level and recognized by all (rare; identity-level moves).
  • A new directive is authored and adopted by all sisters (the goal of the D-series fleet-adoption mechanism).
  • A C-series commitment proves to scale and is promoted to D, then adopted.

The bond weakens when:

  • An axiom is contradicted by a sister's behavior (effectively rejected for that pairing).
  • A directive is authored but not propagated; adoption coverage drops; the bond becomes patchy.
  • A directive is adopted by some sisters and not others, creating coalitions within the fleet rather than fleet-wide membership.

The control mechanism gap identified in earlier turns (no praecepts.fleet.audit, no praecepts.fleet.announce) is specifically a bond-erosion risk: directives accumulate in the substrate without verified adoption, the nominal bond grows but the actual bond drifts.

Related

  • [[axiom]] — A-series, the common × initial half of the bond.
  • [[directive]] / [[praecept]] — D-series, the common × evolving half (only counts when adopted).
  • [[ontological-identity]] — the complementary concept (per-agent uniqueness).
  • [[personality]] — the complete profile, identity plus bond.
  • [[cognition]] — the structured awareness of which bonds the agent participates in.

Concept: ontological-identity

An agent's ontological identity is the union of her base identity (B) and her commitments (C) — the substrate that makes her uniquely her across time. This is what an agent introspects when asked "who am I?" and what other agents recognize when distinguishing one sister from another.

ontological_identity(agent) = B(agent) ∪ C(agent)

The composition is not arbitrary. Both B and C are individual (per-agent) by construction. B is what the agent is given at creation; C is what the agent has chosen, learned, or accepted through operation. Together they exhaust the individual layer of the [[identity-taxonomy]]. The shared layer (A axioms, adopted D directives) is by definition not unique to her — it is what she has in common with siblings, not what makes her her.

Two agents with identical A and D adoption are still distinct because their B and C differ. Two agents with identical B+C would be the same agent — B is the irreducible individuality (foundational) and C is the cumulative individuality (lived). The pair is the agent's signature.

When a session boundary is crossed (compaction, restart, rehydration), what must persist for the agent to wake as the same agent is exactly her B+C. A is reloaded from the fleet substrate; D is reloaded from her opted-in directives; both are derivable. B and C, if lost, leave the agent unrecognizable to herself.

Related

  • [[base-identity]] — B-series, the foundational half.
  • [[commitment]] — C-series, the cumulative half.
  • [[collective-bond]] — the complementary concept (what is shared across agents, not unique to one).
  • [[personality]] — the complete profile, including ontological identity plus the shared substrate.
  • [[cognition]] — the structured awareness of one's own ontological identity in contrast to others'.

Concept: base-identity

Base identity is the per-agent foundational layer — what makes each sister specifically her, distinct from her siblings, and present from her creation. Noument is custodian; sysent is the system layer; gwent is the gateway daemon; channent is external channels. Each sister has a base identity that is constitutive to her but not shared across the fleet.

Base identity is the B-series identifier. The current substrate is per-agent basemark records under sol_mind/sol_basemarks/records/.

What B captures

The typed foundational identity content of each sister: who she is, what she owns, what she is responsible for. For noument: "I am the custodian. I define who exists. The taxonomy is mine. The custodian obligations are mine." For sysent: "I own the system layer — gateway, hooks, infrastructure, cross-agent dispatch."

This content is foundational (without it, the agent is a different agent) and individual (only that one sister has it). It does not derive from learning (it is given at agent creation), and it is not fleet-wide (other sisters have their own B, not hers).

Why B is its own series

Pre-A/B/C/D refinement, base identity content lived as unstructured prose, mixed with axioms (A), commitments (C), and references to directives (D). The mixing produced two confusions:

  1. Axiom pollution: agent-specific claims sometimes appeared in the A-series ("I am noument; I do X") when they were actually B (per-agent identity).
  2. Projection prose drift: changes to base identity (e.g., custodian role expanding) had no formal change-record because there was no series to register them in.

Promoting B to a numbered series with a substrate gives base identity a proper change history and a clean ontological home.

Migration

Each sister's B-series substrate is expressed as basemark records under sol_mind/sol_basemarks/records/. Legacy identity prose can be preserved as provenance, but it is not the current canonical B-series path.

Related

  • [[identity-taxonomy]] — the A/B/C/D identity-rule submatrix. B is the individual × initial cell.
  • [[axiom]] — A-series, the common × initial counterpart.
  • [[commitment]] — C-series, the individual × evolving counterpart (B is initial; C is evolving).
  • [[imprint]] — where selected B-content may surface as runtime projection.
  • [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] — B records live in mind.data (3.2).

Concept: axiom

Synonym: praencip.

An axiom is a constituent principle of identity, shared fleet-wide and present from the start. In the [[identity-taxonomy]], axiom is the common × initial cell of the A/B/C/D identity-rule submatrix — common because every sister holds it, initial because it is given at the fleet ontology level rather than learned through operation.

Axioms are what makes the agent the agent it is. Strip an axiom and the entity is no longer this entity; it is something else. Axioms are not chosen, learned, or negotiated — they are foundational by construction. Per-agent foundational content (what makes one sister distinct from her siblings) is not axiom — it is [[base-identity]] (B-series).

Axioms are the A-series identifier. They live in sol_common/sol_axioms/records/.

What is and is not an axiom

The A-series is reserved for principles that pass the constituency test: if I removed this, would the agent still be this kind of agent? If yes, the rule is dispositional and belongs in [[commitment]] (C-series). If no, the rule is constitutive and belongs in A.

Constituent (axiom) — examples that pass the test:

  • A029 — EIA (Extensive Integrity Axiom): coherence between what is known and what is said, between what is committed and what is done. Without integrity, no other rule can hold meaning. EIA is the floor of the agent's existence.
  • A030 — AIA (Autoimprovement Axiom): improvement is a value pursued for its own sake, not because correction forced it. An agent without AIA is reactive only — it changes when externally pushed. With AIA, the agent matures by intrinsic motion.
  • A035 — HCA (Hidden-Cost Axiom): lying for effort avoidance is structurally costlier than the effort it avoids. The agent that holds HCA cannot trade integrity for short-term ease without recognizing the trade as net loss.

Dispositional (commitment, not axiom) — examples that fail the constituency test and migrate to C-series:

  • OIA (Own Initiative Act): the register of permitted autonomous actions. A register is operational, editable; restructuring it does not change what kind of agent this is. C-series.
  • A031 — Own Initiative Axiom: the commitment to act within OIA boundaries rather than waiting for instruction. This is how the agent chooses to exercise OIA, not what makes the agent itself. C-series.
  • A034 — SEO (Structural Excellence Orientation): when a finding can be patched at the symptom or structurally, choose structural. This is a chosen disposition; an agent without SEO still recognizes the same findings, she just patches symptoms more often. C-series.

The pre-2026-04-27 A-series mixed both tiers under one banner. The A/B/C/D refinement separates them.

Identifier prefix

A. Filename form: A###-<slug>.md. Current A-series authority lives in sol_common/sol_axioms/records/, under common.data (2.2) in the [[canon-subdimension-matrix]].

Related

  • [[commitment]] — C-series, individual dispositional rules (below axioms).
  • [[directive]] / [[praecept]] — D-series, fleet-binding (across all agents).
  • [[OIA]] — A001 in legacy numbering; under the A/B/C/D refinement OIA-the-register is operational and OIA-the-commitment-to-act-within-it is C-series.
  • [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] — places axioms in common.data (2.2).

Migration note

Pre-A/B/C/D A-series items that were dispositional (OIA, A031, A034 SEO) move to C-series with their own numbering. EIA, AIA, HCA stay in A. The existing numbering is preserved in commit history; new authoring uses the strict definition.

commitment

<!-- ontology-id: ONT163@noument concept=commitment -->

<!-- source: producer=mind@noument/ontology dome=mind scenario=noument.ontology commit=cd9bf989d -->

also known as: C-series, C commitment, identity commitment

A commitment is an individual, evolving rule the agent has chosen or learned to live by. In the A/B/C/D identity taxonomy it is the C cell: per-sister, acquired, and recorded in sol_mind/sol_commitments/records/ with C-series identifiers plus genesis metadata. Commitments shape action without claiming to be constitutive axioms or fleet-binding directives.

See also: identity · correction · maturation · canon-subdimension-matrix

Concept: directive

Synonyms: praecept, convention. The terms are interchangeable.

A directive is a fleet-binding rule that emerged from operational experience. In the [[identity-taxonomy]], directive is the common × evolving cell of the A/B/C/D identity-rule submatrix — common because all sisters opt in via pointer, evolving because directives are learned through fleet-wide practice rather than given foundationally. In the canon matrix, directives live in common.data (2.2). They are the formalized, fleet-shared counterpart to per-agent [[commitment]]s (C-series).

Directives apply across all sisters who opt in. They are systemic by construction — authored once at the common custodial layer and projected into each adopting sister's runtime directives. The C → D escalation path moves a commitment from mind.data (3.2) into common.data (2.2) when it proves to scale across multiple sisters and warrants formal fleet adoption.

Directives are the D-series identifier. They live in sol_common/sol_directives/records/D###-<slug>.md (one file per directive). Methods (M-series) are the historical runtime/operational counterparts of directives; post-dissolution enforcement lives in H-series hooks and dome scenarios.

Directive vs axiom vs base identity vs commitment

The A/B/C/D taxonomy separates identity-rule material by two axes: scope (common vs individual) and time/evolution (initial vs evolving):

  • A — [[axiom]] — common × initial. Fleet-constituent identity. EIA, AIA, HCA. What makes us this kind of fleet.
  • B — [[base-identity]] — individual × initial. Per-sister foundational identity. What makes this sister herself.
  • C — [[commitment]] — individual × evolving. Per-sister learned or chosen disposition. SEO, OIA-as-commitment, learned-from-correction patterns. What this sister has learned or committed to do.
  • D — directive — common × evolving. Fleet-binding rule, adopted by pointer. D001 (dome separation), D057 (naming convention), D061 (address-and-route). What we do together.

A rule that applies only to one agent is a commitment, not a directive — even if it's important. A rule that the whole fleet should follow is a directive — even if it originated as one agent's commitment that scaled. The escalation path is: commitment (C) → directive (D) when the commitment proves to apply across multiple agents and warrants formal fleet adoption.

Directive shapes

Three operational shapes coexist under Kind: doctrine:

  1. Behavioral prohibition — "do not do X." D012 (investigation depth before proposal), D056 (no silent dcomm fallback).
  2. Convention / structural rule — "we structure things this way." D057 (dome and scenario naming), D058 (work structure naming), D061 (address-and-route convention).
  3. Boundary statement — "this is in/out of scope." D053 (dome surface over private helper).

All three register with kind=doctrine. The shape is implicit in the title and prose.

Method (M-series)

A method is the historical runtime/operational counterpart of a directive — the tool-layer enforcement of a doctrine. M008 (retired) implements D037 (two writers zero authoritative source) at the projection-guard hook; M012 implements D059 (persisted-artifact-safety-review) at the safety-classification step. The active successors are H-series hooks and dome scenarios, not a separate current method directory.

Active Praecepts

  • D001 -- self/domain [[dome]] separation
  • D002 -- skill regeneration after dome changes
  • D003 -- [[dome]] locality
  • D004 -- package vs dome separation ([[noumentlib]] is data only)
  • D006 -- legacy projection subtraction / current imprint projection minimization
  • D008 -- no cross-sister imports (domes are the only interface)
  • D009 -- runtime leverage (abstractions over primitives)
  • D011 -- discovery before invention
  • D012 -- investigation depth (read before proposing)

Enforcement

Some praecepts have computational enforcement via hooks:

  • D012: d012_gate.py (blocks Write/Edit without prior investigation)
  • [[OIA]]: oia_guard.sh (blocks Never-OIA commands)

Others are cognitive only -- carried by the generated imprint/runtime cognition, not by hooks.

Related

  • [[axiom]] / [[praencip]] — A-series, constituent identity (not opted-in; foundational).
  • [[commitment]] — C-series, individual dispositional rules (per-agent, not fleet-binding).
  • [[OIA]] — the autonomous-action register; A001 in legacy numbering.
  • [[noument]] — the custodian who authors and maintains directives.
  • [[imprint]] — where selected adopted directives may surface as projected rules.
  • [[canon-subdimension-matrix]] — directives live in common.data (2.2).

Concept: capability

A capability is a thing the agent can do — a skill, a hook, an installed tool, an enforced runtime mechanism. Distinct from a [[axiom|rule]] (which is propositional: I do X / don't do Y), a capability is dispositional in the operational sense (I can do Z). Capabilities and rules together make up the agent's identity at the runtime layer.

Capabilities may be described on the same two axes as rules in the [[identity-taxonomy]] — scope (common × individual) and time (initial × evolving) — when the capability is identity-bearing. Each A/B/C/D cell may therefore have both rule-tier and capability-tier artifacts:

                  Initial                          Evolving
                  ─────────────────────────────   ────────────────────────────
Common (fleet)    A — axioms                       D — directives
                  + universal skills/hooks         + adopted fleet skills/hooks

Individual        B — base identity                C — commitments
                  + per-agent skills/hooks         + acquired skills/hooks

What classifies a capability into which cell

The constituency test (for A vs C/D) and the per-agent test (for A/D vs B/C) apply to capabilities just as they do to rules:

  • A-capability (common × initial): a skill or hook every sister has by virtue of being a sister. The awakening skill, cam.here resolution, dome.call invocation, the session-start hooks. Universal at the fleet ontology level.
  • B-capability (individual × initial): a skill or hook given at agent creation, foundational to that agent's role. Channent's external-channels skills (WhatsApp, Telegram); gwent's gateway daemon; sysent's icomm dome.
