Concept: legacy disposition model layer

ONT013 is a retired compatibility record for the older disposition formulation that centered the model layer. The active ontology term is [[disposition]] in ONT098, which generalizes disposition as clengine plus capability plus qmodels. Keep this row only as provenance for older references to ONT013.

Disposition is the COMPOUND behavioural layer determined by clengine + constituent qmodels that an agent runs on. It is not who the agent is and not what she has learned. It is how she behaves given the runtime environment AND the per-capability model assignments instantiating her.

Corrected per N4194 (twin s001 ratification 2026-05-24): the prior version of this entry treated qmodel as the sole determinant; that was incomplete. Disposition is composite:

  • Clengine — the runtime environment that hosts the agent (codex CLI, Claude Code, Hermes runtime, future engines). Different clengines have different tool surfaces, context-management semantics, prompt-injection paths, and hook lifecycles. The agent INSIDE codex behaves differently from the same agent INSIDE Claude Code even with identical qmodel assignments, because the surrounding execution environment is different.

  • Constituent qmodels — within a clengine, multiple capabilities may use different models. Today's substrate exposes capabilities.thinking.qmodel, capabilities.acting.qmodel, potentially others. Example: a sister might run on Codex CLI (clengine=codex) with thinking via claude~cp:thinking~tr:flagship (Anthropic Opus) and acting via openai~cp:thinking~tr:flagship (OpenAI). Two clengine-orthogonal selections that together fully specify the per-capability model layer.

Formal definition:

disposition = (clengine, { capability → qmodel })

The same [[identity]] and the same [[learning]]s, on a different (clengine, qmodel-map) tuple, produce a different disposition. A smaller qmodel is terser; a larger qmodel reaches for more structure; a reasoning-tuned qmodel thinks longer before answering; different providers default to different framings; different clengines impose different tool/hook/context constraints. All of these are dispositional differences, not identity differences.

Selector property

The selector for the clengine layer is --clengine (one of codex / claude / hermes / future). Substrate code uses clengine as the precise property name throughout. The historical synonym --runtime is deprecated as a selector name because it is ambiguous (could mean clengine, could mean the currently-running process, could mean the substrate execution layer). All disposition-related scenarios should use --clengine.

Why the concept earns its place

Without disposition as a named layer, behavioural change caused by a qmodel swap gets misread as identity drift. The custodian would attempt to reconcile identity when nothing about identity had actually moved. Naming disposition lets the system distinguish the agent is now different (identity or learnings changed) from the model under the agent is different (disposition changed). The first requires [[maturation]]; the second requires a [[reconciliation]] sweep to verify the new qmodel still respects the unchanged identity and learnings.

Observable, not constitutive

Disposition is observable — it surfaces in tone, pacing, structural preferences, default length, default framings. It is not constitutive. An agent's axioms, commitments, and learned patterns do not live here. This is why disposition is the right name: it points at the surface, not the core. It is the shape the model imposes on everything else the agent is.

Relationship to qmodel

The qmodel layer is ONE constituent of disposition (the per-capability layer); clengine is the other. A qmodel swap for any capability changes disposition automatically; a clengine swap changes it more deeply. There is no separate root "disposition file" to edit — disposition is the composite of (clengine, qmodel-map), with the qmodel-map sourced from profile records under sol_mind/sol_awakening/records/ and clengine selection driven by which imprint a process boots with at E1. Attempts to "tune disposition" either change one of these constituents (acceptable — substrate swap) or write into identity records or learnings under the guise of disposition work (misplaced — those edits belong to identity or learnings, not here).

Substrate-of-record schema

Per-sister disposition source lives in PROF-series profile records under sol_mind/sol_awakening/records/. Legacy root identity.yaml is not a disposition source.

Canonical shape:

agent: <sister-id>
disposition:
  clengine: <claude|codex|gemini|hermes|...>
  capabilities:
    <capability-name>:                # e.g. thinking, acting, coding
      qmodel: <provider>~cp:<capability>~tr:<tier>  # e.g. claude~cp:thinking~tr:flagship
      provider: <provider-id>         # optional; inferred from qmodel
      auth_preference: [...]          # optional
      notes: |                        # optional rationale
        ...

The same sister's capabilities top-level list (e.g., [audit_fleet, register_agents, ...]) coexists with disposition.capabilities map; they are distinct concepts (per ONT167 — capabilities-as-things-the-agent-can-do vs the disposition map which is per-capability qmodel binding).

Imprint section format

When concite composes disposition into a clengine's imprint (Task 15 items 3-5), the section shape depends on imprint format:

Markdown imprints (CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md, hermes SOUL.md):

## DISPOSITION
```yaml
clengine: claude
capabilities:
  thinking:
    qmodel: claude~cp:thinking~tr:flagship
    provider: anthropic
  acting:
    qmodel: claude~cp:thinking~tr:flagship
    provider: anthropic
```
<!-- Generated by projection at <ISO-ts>; source: <sister>/sol_mind/sol_awakening/records/PROF*.yaml -->

JSON imprints (hermes nouments-imprint.json):

{
  "disposition": {
    "clengine": "hermes",
    "capabilities": {
      "thinking": {"qmodel": "claude~cp:thinking~tr:flagship", "provider": "anthropic"}
    },
    "_meta": {
      "generated_at": "<ISO-ts>",
      "source": "<sister>/sol_mind/sol_awakening/records/PROF*.yaml"
    }
  }
}

The section's job is to make disposition VISIBLE in the imprint that E1 reads, so the running clengine has its disposition directly inspectable from its own loaded context (today disposition is implicit in which-imprint-was-loaded for clengine, and not loaded at all for the qmodel-map; with the section, the full compound is set at E1).

Mutation surfaces (current + owed)

  • capability.qmodel.change --agent=<her> --capability=<X> --qmodel=<Y> — mutates ONE entry in the qmodel-map layer through the capability/profile service. Backs up the PROF-series source record, updates the selected profile qmodel, handles cross-provider auth, and reconciles affected projections. This covers the qmodel-map layer of disposition.

  • capability.clengine.change --agent=<her> --clengine=<Y> — OWED companion scenario for the clengine layer of disposition. Today there is no canonical write surface for the clengine selection; cross-clengine moves require manual substrate edits. Per N4194 item 6 (Task 15).

Imprint encoding (current gap + owed)

Disposition SHOULD be composed into each clengine's imprint at projection time — a DISPOSITION section in CLAUDE.md / AGENTS.md / SOUL.md that names the clengine and the qmodel-map at the moment of projection. Today's imprints do not include such a section; the disposition is implicit in WHICH imprint is being read (clengine) plus separate substrate reads of the profile source (qmodel-map). Per N4194 item 3-5 (Task 15), projection gains the responsibility to compose disposition explicitly; awakening verification gains the corresponding read-and-compare surface.

Related

  • [[identity]] — what disposition is distinguished from
  • [[qmodels]] — the determinant of disposition
  • [[learning]] — also distinguished; learnings persist across qmodel changes, disposition does not
  • [[imprint]] — where disposition may be projected for runtime visibility; disposition is not identity
  • [[reconciliation]] — the custodian verb that runs after a qmodel change to confirm identity and learnings still project cleanly onto the new disposition

Concept: canon coordinates are dim.level.facet — numbers locate, the letter classifies

A canon coordinate is <dim>.<level>.<facet-letter>: the dimension and the level are NUMBERS, the facet is a LETTER, and the letter terminates the coordinate.

dim:    0 root · 1 basis · 2 common · 3 mind · 4 work · 5 domain
level:  0 = the dimension node itself · 1 = its members · 2 = members-of-members
facet:  a struct · b logic · c data · d other   (ordinal-preserving: a↔0 … d↔3)

Why letters: the retired grammar (N.facet, both numeric) overloaded the digits three ways — dimension 0 (root), facet 0 (struct), and the recursive current-node self-reference — and had NO level position at all: "basis, level 1" was unwritable (the 1.1.0 failure that forced this). With letters, every digit is a where and the letter is a what; depth becomes explicit instead of narrated by a recursive rule.

Selectors: * per position — 1.*.a (basis struct at every level), *.*.b (all logic everywhere), 1.1.* (all facets of basis level 1). The AUTHORITY matrix cells are level-wildcards: matrix[dim][facet] governs dim.*.letter.

Names locate, coordinates classify (ONT252): a coordinate quantifies over ALL members at its level; a specific item is path + coordinate. In control surfaces the middle position may hold a scope NAME (2.sol_ontology.b) — a name in the middle locates, a number or * classifies.

Examples: 0.0.a the mentspace root node itself · 0.1.a root members struct (the universality-law item set) · 1.1.a basis level-1 struct (the pack-dome names) · 1.2.b logic inside each basis pack dome · 4.2.c data records of each work concept.

Numeric facets fail loud (D146): the old forms are not aliased and do not resolve; the resolver's error names the letter mapping. History and receipts that say 2.1 truthfully name what was (J113 class C/D) and are not rewritten.

Concept: loop

A loop is a verifiable recurring composition: work that fires repeatedly over current state, records a receipt each firing, and is accepted only by an independent verifier — never by its own claim of success. Its skeleton (N4663):

TRIGGER → ACT (over current state) → RECORD (receipt) → check STOP → repeat

Continuity lives in the receipt, not in the agent: each firing forgets, the record does not. That asymmetry is the whole architecture.

A loop is NOT a dome — it is a composition across five layers

Ruled in N4683/N4695 and re-affirmed by the N4977 review. The workflow-family domes are not competing systems; they are the orthogonal layers a loop composes:

layer question owning surface
1 TRIGGER what wakes it? cron (time, ONT215) · hook (event) · rhythm (internal impulse, ONT186)
2 SHAPE how is the work shaped? workflow TYPE (ONT219; workflow_type=loop absorbed SLOOP per N4830)
3 CONTRACT what binds and accepts it? common.workflows.loop_contract.v1 — ⟨pre→act→post, RECEIPT, independent VERIFIER, STOP + max-iteration backstop⟩
4 EXECUTION where do instances live? the OWNING work object (<J>/plan/workflow.json, service state, MON/CRON obs) — never a central instance register
5 VERIFY who accepts a run? an independent, live-proven verifier over a fresh receipt (self-observation is evidence, not certification)

The trigger and the logic are always separated: the cron/hook is the doorbell; the ACT logic lives in a dome/scenario/worker (N4663 invariant).

Discriminators (the family, one line each)

  • loop vs cron: the cron is only the loop's TIME doorbell (layer 1); a cron with no contract/verifier is a schedule, not a loop.
  • loop vs monitor (ONT227): a monitor is a standing UNBOUNDED loop species specialized for observation — cadence + process call + followup call; its watch condition replaces the STOP bound.
  • loop vs campaign (ONT222): a campaign is a BOUNDED loop species — cadence + count-bound + roster ledger; it self-terminates when the bound is met.
  • loop vs rhythm (ONT186): a rhythm is the INTERNAL cognitive trigger species (self-originated cadence of the mind); it may wake loops but carries no contract.
  • loop vs workflow (ONT219): workflow is the SHAPE grammar (layer 2); loop is one workflow_type. A workflow type without trigger+contract is a pattern, not a loop.
  • loop vs service (ONT046): a service is the INVOCATION CONTRACT for a capability (manual/trigger/cron routes); a service invoked on cadence with a verifier becomes a loop's layers 1+3 without owning the concept.
  • loop vs libreto (ONT218): a libreto choreographs MULTI-AGENT performance; a loop is actor-agnostic recurrence. A libreto may orchestrate loop firings.

Acceptance (the non-negotiable)

A loop run is ACCEPTED iff an independent, live-proven verifier passes on a fresh receipt: not the same actor/session/loop as the emitter; live-proof by substance (request correlation + receiver receipt + response receipt + ledger ingest), never by a declared boolean. Cross-clengine judging (verifier_clengine ≠ emitter.clengine) is the strongest independence tier. Full contract: workflow.loop.contract.

History (why this row exists)

The concept operated untyped from J074 (2026-06-16) to 2026-07-10 while its definition lived in a closed project's outputs and three activity notes — the recurring "how do our domes relate to loops" discussion was the symptom (A041: a word not bound to a typed entry carries only private intention). Typed on the operator's second ask, same day the contract was promoted (T1042).

Concept: monitor

A monitor is a work operation, usually represented by a MON### record, that repeatedly checks a declared condition and turns the result into followup work. It is the work-substrate artifact for the broader concept [[monitoring]] (ONT035).

A monitor is not live merely because it has a description, and it is not complete merely because it detects something. A maintained monitor has three required features:

  1. Cadence / runtime scheduler - the runtime authority that decides when the monitor fires. This may be a cron record, gateway job, launchd projection, recurrence, or another declared scheduler. The cadence must be inspectable as runtime truth, not only as prose intent.

  2. Process dome call - the actual measurement/check call that runs at the cadence. The process names the dome scenario, inputs, scope, and output receipt. It produces the raw verdict material: what was checked, what was covered, and what evidence exists.

  3. Followup dome call - the deterministic processing of the process output. Followup reads the receipt/result and then records the signal, updates monitor state, opens or updates tasks, notifies the responsible owner, or performs an allowed corrective action. Followup is based on the process output; it must not invent a clean result when process evidence is absent.

Completeness rule

A monitor is structurally incomplete if any of the three features is missing:

  • cadence without process means something may fire, but nothing is proved;
  • process without cadence means the check is callable but not maintained;
  • process without followup means detection exists but correction does not;
  • followup without a process receipt is ungrounded action.

The monitor's emitted [[signal]] (ONT225) must fail closed: absent cadence proof, zero coverage, missing process receipt, or missing followup evidence cannot be reported as PASS.

Relation to operation and monitoring

monitor (ONT227) is the work artifact/species. monitoring (ONT035) is the general verification concern. operation (ONT224) provides the genus rule: triggered work emits a signal. signal (ONT225) supplies the fail-closed output contract.

Ontology maintenance as a monitorable example

The ontology maintenance pipeline has its own three phase shape:

  1. Process input / sources - collect and classify ontology item sources: producer-backed rows, hand-curated rows, external/proposed rows, retired rows, and unknown rows that must block until repaired.

  2. Consolidate - reconcile admitted inputs into meta.json:concept_registry plus records/ONT*.md, bind row hashes, reject duplicate concepts, and verify registry/body coherence.

  3. Disseminate - render ontology.md, expose awake-time retrieval references, and hand off publication only when publish-ready evidence exists.

A monitor over ontology maintenance should therefore use a cadence/runtime scheduler to call the ontology maintenance process, use the process output from o.maintenance.model or o.release.reconcile, and run a followup call that opens remediation work or requests publication based on that output.

Related

  • [[monitoring]] (ONT035) - broader continuous verification concern
  • [[operation]] (ONT224) - work-unit genus
  • [[signal]] (ONT225) - fail-closed output contract
  • [[cron]] (ONT215) - scheduler-neutral cadence definition
  • [[gateway]] (ONT226) - runtime scheduling authority
  • sol_work/sol_monitors/ - monitor records and verification/followup surfaces

monitors

<!-- ontology-id: ONT229@noument concept=monitors -->

<!-- source: producer=work@noument/ontology dome=work scenario=do.ontology commit=cd9bf989d -->

also known as: sol_monitors, sol_work/sol_monitors, MON

monitors is the sol_work/sol_monitors/ concept for MON-series collective monitors. Each MON declares one observation contract: what is watched, when it fires, what threshold counts as breach, what proof_of_property is required, where the alert routes, what correction follows, and which W-record or owner scenario (if any) enforces.

Per 3-way convergence (msg arc 2026-05-21 23:30Z-23:45Z) between s020/ s011/s028:

  • MON observes + routes + receipts; W/G or owner-scenario enforces+ corrects. The pairing IS the safety guarantee.
  • coverage_class is MANDATORY (5 values): MON+W | W_only | MON_only_incomplete | owed_not_wired | not_applicable_retired
  • Three-part test (per provent/ntent N049): record exists + monitor fires + safety property proven. Only the third is PASS.

Concept: one dome, one address

One dome, one address is the resolver contract ruled by doment (xsol surface owner, 2026-07-08): a dome resolves at exactly one sol address. Dome-level symlinks inside a dimension are deliberately opaque to address discovery.

Rationale:

  • Route invariance — following dome-level links would make one dome resolvable at N addresses; aliases never die and callers never migrate. The healthy move pattern is atomic reference migration (all live callers updated in the move commit, DEC038 precedent).
  • Hermeticity — a dome-level link can point anywhere, including a sister's source tree; following it in discovery reinvites the load-bearing-external-path failure class (SOL-PKG005 incident, same day).

The documented asymmetry: dimension-level symlinks (a sister's whole sol_basis/sol_common into noument) remain sanctioned canon entanglement (ONT246) and DO resolve. The contract binds dome-level links only.

Moved-dome UX: the old-location symlink stays as a tombstone — resolver failure over an existing symlink emits a loud address.dome_moved naming the successor derived from the link target (fail-with-forwarding, never follow; doment T054). Silent dome_not_found for a moved address is indistinguishable from a typo and is therefore a defect.

Concept: ontokey

An ontokey is an admitted ontoterm selected for mandatory compact first-load projection into every generated runtime imprint. It names ontology knowledge that must be present before retrieval because missing it repeatedly causes substrate confusion, wrong authority claims, or unsafe routing.

Ontokey is not a second ontology authority. Authority remains sol_common/sol_ontology/meta.json:concept_registry plus the content-hash-bound records/ONT*.md rows. Ontokey is a projection selector over admitted ontology authority.

Selection rule

An ontology row is an ontokey only when the authoritative registry entry marks it as imprint_required: true.

The selected row must already be an admitted ontoterm, normally state=hand_curated. Producer-backed ontodos cannot become ontokeys directly: producer material must first be consolidated into an ontoterm, with custody and hash binding, before it can be selected for mandatory imprint projection.

Projection rule

The imprint projects ontokeys compactly, not as full row bodies. Each projected ontokey should carry:

  • id and concept name,
  • one-line title or meaning,
  • source authority path,
  • retrieval surface for the full row.

Full ontology prose remains on demand through o.show, o.list, o.registry, o.status, and the owning producer surfaces where applicable.

Management relation

Ontokeys are governed by the same ontologize process as other ontology changes:

  1. Sources/inputs propose or identify a candidate.
  2. Consolidation admits or updates the underlying ontoterm.
  3. Dissemination projects the selected ontokeys into runtime imprints and verifies their presence.

The test for an ontokey is therefore computational: a fresh runtime should contain the compact ontokey line in its generated imprint without being taught the term by the prompt that tests it.

Related

  • [[imprint]] — runtime projection target.
  • [[imprint-essential-set-selection]] — general selection discipline for first-load material.
  • [[ontology-maintenance]] — process that admits and disseminates ontology terms.
  • [[canonical-registry]] — registry authority that carries the selector.

ontology-fragment

<!-- ontology-id: ONT161@noument concept=ontology-fragment -->

<!-- source: producer=mind@noument/ontology dome=mind scenario=noument.ontology commit=cd9bf989d -->

also known as: D043 fragment, fragment contract, six-field schema

A D043-shaped unit of ontological content owned by one dome. Six fields: concept (required, the h2 anchor), body (required, prose), aliases (optional list of alternative names for the alias map), code_refs (optional dotted references resolved via AST walk to module-level names), subsections (optional list of nested fragments with the same contract shape recursively, up to depth 3 inline before link-out), related (optional list of sibling concepts for cross-reference). Producers are <dome>.ontology scenarios; the composer discovers them via ments catalog, invokes each in isolation, validates against the schema, detects collisions, and renders ontology.md.

See also: scenario · dome

Concept: ontology maintenance

Ontology maintenance is the workflow by which proposed ontology changes become maintained ontology canon. It answers the question "how does a proposed concept become accepted into the canonical registry, and how is the registry kept consistent over time?".

It is a process concept — a sequence of substrate-affecting steps with named roles and verification gates — not a content concept. The maintenance workflow is the operational counterpart to [[canonical-registry]] (which is the STATIC schema-of-forms); maintenance is the DYNAMIC discipline that updates the registry safely.

Roles

  • Proposer — any sister may propose a new row, amendment, retirement, split, merge, or section change. A proposer may carry their own ment-side ontology fragment OR a paired companion row at their substrate; the proposal flow accommodates ment-local fragments upstream of registry consolidation.
  • Concept owner — the ment or domain responsible for the meaning of the concept; reviews semantic correctness and future drift. Named in the registry owner field. Concept owner review is real, not ceremonial — noument has registry custody, NOT semantic monopoly.
  • Registry custodian — noument maintains the collective registry at sol_common/sol_ontology/. Custodial, not decree authority.
  • Auditor — U001 verifies mentspace/canonization structure; the envelope contract for o.verify (substrate at this ONT row's parent folder) verifies registry/body consistency. Implementation mechanics live in dome_pkg per D003 dome-locality; per-sister scenario wrappers invoke the mechanics locally.

Three-phase maintenance model

Ontology maintenance is controlled through three computational phases:

  1. Sources / inputs — collect and classify inputs before they can affect authority. Inputs are split into two named streams. Ontodos are producer-backed ontology fragments emitted by owning dome producer surfaces; their current registry selector is state=producer_backed, and their semantic owner remains the producer dome. Ontoterms are ontology terms authored, curated, or explicitly accepted by noument ontology custody; their current registry selector is state=hand_curated, and raw operator, sister, audit, or external inputs become ontoterms only after OR/OP intake and consolidation. The key evidence is source-class counts, transition-action counts, effective producer discovery, unresolved classifications, and legacy producer refs.

  2. Consolidation — reconcile accepted inputs into one authoritative substrate: meta.json:concept_registry plus records/ONT*.md prose rows bound by content_hash. The key evidence is registry/body verification, duplicate-concept checks, manifest checks, and release-reconcile consolidation status.

  3. Dissemination — project the maintained ontology into downstream surfaces: generated ontology.md, awake-time knowledge-substrate references, local nousite, and public nouments.com. Dissemination never changes authority; it only proves that derived surfaces match authority. The key evidence is render-current status, publish-ready handoff state, local nousite status, and public site health.

Ontoterms, ontodos, and imprint projection

The ontology authority must keep vocabulary custody and producer discovery separate:

  • Ontoterms are the curated ontology vocabulary. They are the concepts a sister should carry as compact first-load cognition: the names, management process, authority surfaces, and retrieval routes needed to answer ontology questions or route ontology work. They live as admitted ONT rows in meta.json:concept_registry plus records/ONT*.md, and are governed by sol_common/sol_ontology/do.py and the ontology schemas.
  • Ontokeys are the subset of ontoterms marked imprint_required: true. They are not a new authority layer; they are the mandatory compact first-load selection that the imprint must carry at every wake.
  • Ontodos are producer-backed dome ontology outputs. They are an annex-like source inventory: useful evidence that an owning dome can supply ontology material, but not a transfer of semantic custody to noument ontology merely because the fragment was discovered. They are collected, classified, and checked by ontology maintenance, while their detailed meaning remains governed by the producing dome until consolidation explicitly admits or merges a term.

The generated clengine imprint uses the same boundary:

  • Ontoterms: include compactly. The imprint may include a compact ontoterm index, management-process vocabulary, current-status surfaces, and retrieval pointers. This is enough for runtime cognition to know what the ontology is and how to use it.
  • Ontokeys: must include compactly. The imprint must include every registry row marked imprint_required: true, as id, concept, title/meaning, authority path, and retrieval surface. This is the part that must always be present in mind before retrieval.
  • Ontodos: reference only. The imprint may say producer-backed ontodos are available and name discovery/fetch routes such as o.discover, o.collect, o.fetch, and the owning dome. It must not preload producer fragment bodies.
  • Full ontology row bodies are not first-load material. A sister reads the prompt plus compact imprint first; if detail is needed, she retrieves current authority with o.status, o.show, o.list, or producer discovery/fetch surfaces.

In canon-matrix terms, admitted ontology records are common.data (2.2) while the selectors, schemas, validators, source collection, consolidation, and projection rules are common.logic (2.1). Ontodos may originate in any owning dome's producer logic/data; ontology maintenance indexes them before it chooses whether to consolidate any part into common ontology authority.

The computable control surface is:

sol noument/common/ontology/o.maintenance.model --format=json
sol noument/common/ontology/o.ontologize --format=json

o.maintenance.model returns schema: o.maintenance.model.v1, the three phases in order, phase-level evidence, blockers, and next actions. It is the preferred monitor input because it composes o.source_classes, o.verify, o.status, duplicate-concept checks, and the latest o.release.reconcile receipt.

o.ontologize is the management-process surface: it explains how a candidate term moves from source/input, through consolidation, into dissemination.

Each phase emits step output, not only a cognitive summary. The current computed output fields are:

  • latest_ontologized — the latest accepted ontoterm and ontodo, selected from meta.json:concept_registry by stream state. The ontoterm stream is state=hand_curated; the ontodo stream is state=producer_backed. Each entry names its nature, registry index, id, concept, title, owner, source, content ref/hash, registry lifecycle date fields, and a body excerpt.
  • last_effective_ontologize — the last persisted _tmp/ontology/_ontologize.latest.json handoff date and status. Plain o.ontologize is read-only; o.ontologize.handoff writes the effective process receipt.
  • projection_comparison — the dissemination comparison between the current authoritative render, the local nousite ontology page, public https://nouments.com/ontology/, and the Codex imprint ontology policy.

F1.S6 and F2.S4 include latest_ontologized and last_effective_ontologize in output_data. F3.S5 includes last_effective_ontologize and projection_comparison in output_data. F3 is green only when the local nousite, public nouments.com, and Codex imprint projections are proven current against the maintained ontology.

Canonical ontology row dates, when needed by a computational surface, belong in meta.json:concept_registry as explicit lifecycle fields such as admitted_at, updated_at, and admission_ref. Filesystem metadata is not ontology canon and must not be used as a substitute for missing registry lifecycle fields.

Ontologize proposal intake

Proposed terms that are not already an authoritative ONT row or a pulled producer fragment are saved as pre-authority proposal records:

sol_common/sol_ontology/proposals/OP-<concept-slug>.yaml

The proposal schema is:

sol_common/sol_ontology/schemas/ontology-proposal.schema.yaml

The proposal surfaces are:

sol noument/common/ontology/o.ontologize.propose --concept=<slug> --title=<title> --body=<definition> --rationale=<why> --proposed_id_scheme=ONT|unknown --source_kind=operator|sister|producer|external|repair|unknown
sol noument/common/ontology/o.ontologize.proposals --format=json

Proposal records are not authority. They become authority only if consolidation admits them by writing or updating the corresponding ONT row, updating meta.json:concept_registry, recalculating content_hash, and passing o.verify.

Ontology item source classes

Every ontology item must have an explainable source class before it can be maintained safely:

  • producer_backed — the item is owned by an emitting dome or producer. Consolidation pulls or updates it from producer output.
  • hand_curated — the item is authored directly under noument ontology custody. Consolidation carries the registered row and hash forward.
  • external_identified — the item is externally or provisionally identified. It blocks until intake admits, rejects, merges, or reclassifies it.
  • seed_preserved — the item is preserved from seed/bootstrap material until it is migrated, re-owned, or retired.
  • retired — the item stays in the registry for compatibility/provenance, but is not an active concept.
  • rendered_projection — the item is a derived render/projection. It must not become registry authority.
  • unknown — the item lacks enough source/state metadata to classify. It blocks consolidation until repaired.

o.source_classes --format=json exposes these classes as counts, transition actions, problems, and explanations. o.maintenance.model uses the same source-class evidence as its first phase.

Workflow (10 steps)

  1. Draft — proposer authors proposed ONT row or amendment. Proposal names concept, title, proposed owner, section, state, rationale, and affected rows.

  2. Classify — change is classified per D088:

    • Trivial — typo, formatting, link repair, no semantic change.
    • Substantive — new concept, semantic amendment, section move, owner change, new state meaning, registry field change.
    • Constitutional — changes to ontology maintenance rules, D094/U001 relationship, identifier classes, authority model.

  3. Review — concept owner checks semantic correctness; noument checks registry fit, identifier allocation, duplication, sectioning, D088 procedure applicability.

  4. Conciliate (if needed) — substantive or constitutional changes run the relevant D088 conciliation; trivial may use custodian lazy-consensus or direct repair with transparent note.

  5. Consolidate — noument writes or updates the ONT row under records/, updates meta.json:concept_registry, and recalculates content_hash from the authoritative prose body.

  6. Verify — run o.verify (registry/body consistency); run U001 if the change affects substrate shape, accepted forms, paths, or audit conditions.

  7. Render — regenerate ontology.md from registry plus prose bodies. Do not hand-edit renders as authority.

  8. Release reconcile / publish-ready — run the release reconciliation surface after render. A current o.status proves registry/render currency; it does not by itself prove producer consolidation or downstream publication. Publish-ready requires the stricter release receipt.

  9. Publish / snapshot — commit with provenance citing proposal, D088/CON record if applicable, concept owner review, and verifier results. Mark the change as snapshot-required: pending (collective ontology row) or snapshot-required: not-applicable (per-ment fragment). When batched ontology changes accumulate, custodian composes next CANON-PKG<N> including all pending collective ontology changes. Per-sister materialization/audit downstream verifies that the snapshot is received correctly (per [[canonical-materialization]]).

  10. Maintain — amend by new commit with provenance; deprecate rather than delete unless D088/archival procedure authorizes retirement; preserve prior names/aliases when consumers may still reference them.

Current-version contract

The ontology does not currently use a semver field. Its current version is the live status tuple reported by the ontology dome: last_updated, registry entry count, row-file count, and generated-render sha. The command that proves the tuple is:

sol noument/common/ontology/o.status --format=json

Do not hard-code the current render hash inside this row: this row is part of the rendered ontology, so changing it changes the render hash. Exact current version evidence belongs in o.status output, release receipts, or operator-facing dome documentation refreshed after render. Do not copy any tuple into downstream pages as authority without refreshing o.status; the command is the currentness proof.

Runtime and publication projection

Awakening is a compact projection, not a full ontology load. Generated clengine imprints tell sisters that sol_common/sol_ontology/ is a knowledge substrate, so they know where ontology authority lives. They do not necessarily know the current version/hash at E001 unless the imprint projection explicitly includes compact version evidence. A sister needing the current ontology version must retrieve the substrate or call o.status.

ontology.md, local nousite pages, and public nouments.com pages are downstream renders. They are current only when their owning publication checks say so. For the public site, the relevant surface is solarient's nousite dome:

sol solarient/domain/nousite/nousite.status --format=json
sol solarient/domain/nousite/nousite.health --format=json

As checked on 2026-06-27, noument's local ontology render was current, but local/public nousite ontology publication was not current: the local and public page still reported Last updated: 2026-06-13 and lacked the 2026-06-27 ontology rows. This is a publication gap, not an ontology authority gap.

State vocabulary

Valid ontology row states (defined as enum in sol_common/sol_ontology/meta.json:state_vocabulary):

  • proposed — not yet accepted into registry canon.
  • producer_backed — generated or supplied by an owning dome/producer.
  • hand_curated — manually authored and accepted by custodian process.
  • ratified — passed D088 where required.
  • deprecated — retained for reference; not preferred for new usage.
  • retired — archived or superseded; kept only for historical trace.

Boundary

Ontology maintenance is the PROCESS by which the canonical registry evolves. It is distinct from:

  • [[canonical-registry]] — what the registry IS at any moment.
  • [[canonical-content]] — the records that fill registered forms.
  • The DIRECTIVE binding the workflow ([[D104]]) — D104 says "you must follow this workflow"; ONT092 says "this is what the workflow is".

Related

  • [[canonical-registry]] (ONT087) — the schema that maintenance evolves.
  • D094 — fleet ontology and canon codes.
  • D088 — conciliation governing fleet-shared substrate authoring.
  • D104 — directive binding ontology changes to follow this workflow.
  • N031 — doment finding/proposal that motivated this row.
  • U001 — mentspace conformance audit machinery.

Concept: operation

An operation is a typed unit of agent work that is triggered, runs a lifecycle, and emits a signal. It is the genus of the work stratum; it owns no records and no dome of its own — it is a grouping term whose job is to carry, by inheritance, the two rules every work-kind must obey:

  1. An operation REFERENCES its trigger; it never embeds a scheduler. The cadence lives in a trigger record (cron/rhythm/event); the operation points at it.
  2. An operation emits a contract-bound SIGNAL (ONT225) — validated fail-closed at write-time by the operation's own dome.

The four species (by purpose + signal shape)

species purpose signal shape dome
service (ONT046) produce an effect receipt of effect sol_services (S)
recurrence (ONT223) run on cadence toward a bound ledger progress sol_recurrences (REC)
monitor (ONT227) prove an invariant holds on cadence verdict + coverage + proof + followup sol_monitors (MON)
audit (ONT197) conformance verdict, point-in-time findings + verdict sol_audits (U)

recurrence is itself a sub-genus (species campaign — bounded; watch — unbounded).

Dome rule (why operation has no dome)

A concept gets a dome only if it owns per-instance records. The four species each own records (S/REC/MON/U) and so each has a dome. operation is a genus — it owns no records — so it is an ontological term only, not a dome. The same rule makes signal and gateway dome-less (a contract and an authority, respectively), while cron/rhythm/event keep their trigger domes.

Differentia rulings (the overlaps)

  • monitor vs audit — continuous invariant-proof (coverage over time) vs point-in-time conformance verdict (the U### snapshot). An audit is a one-shot monitor; a monitor is a continuous audit.
  • watch (recurrence) vs monitor — a watch processes a stream (advances a cursor over new items); a monitor asserts an invariant (verdict + proof). A monitor may use a watch, but its essence is the assertion.
  • campaign (recurrence) vs scheduled service — a campaign tracks bounded, stateful, ledger progress (count==N, self-closing); a scheduled service is a stateless fire-each-tick. Bound+ledger ⇒ recurrence; stateless-repeat ⇒ service.

Why the genus matters

The family felt tangled not because there were too many concepts, but because the organizing terms were missing. operation names the family and is the home of the signal contract: every operation, however triggered, emits a signal that cannot report PASS without proving it ran and covered its scope — which is what makes the false-green failure class (a timed-out scanner reporting "0 findings") structurally impossible rather than caught by vigilance.
